Saya sudah keliling dunia cari jam tangan dan tak bisa temukannya. Masuk ke dalam, saya akan berusaha keras cari jam buat istri. Tapi masalahnya, hampir mustahil 'tuk beli Rolex di tokonya secara langsung. Itu karena mereka tak sedia barang untuk dijual. Saya masuk membawa incaran jam tangan yang saya mau dan mereka bilang itu tak tersedia untuk dibeli, tapi mereka akan catat nomor telepon saya dan menelepon jika stoknya ada. Kira-kira jam tangan untuk wanita apa saja yang saat ini ada stoknya? Cuma ada jam pameran. Tak ada yang dijual. Kalau yang pria? Sekali lagi, saya tak punya... modelnya... Apa tidak ada jam tangan yang dijual? Semuanya hanya pameran. Mereka mengklaim bahwa itu terjadi karena jam tangan mereka sangat diminati banyak orang sampai tidak bisa menyiapkan stok apa pun di toko. Tetapi semua ini sebenarnya hanyalah akal-akalan mereka untuk menjual eksklusivitas sebuah merek. Mereka belajar bahwa mereka jauh lebih untung dengan cara menjual daftar tunggu daripada jam tangannya sendiri. Tapi bagaimana mungkin mereka bisa membenarkan harga yang sangat premium itu kalau mereka bahkan tidak sudi untuk menjualnya langsung di toko? Seluruh ide mematok harga premium biasanya ide yang gampang dimengerti oleh banyak pemilik bisnis. Tapi hal yang mereka tak suka pikirkan adalah seperti apa jenis layanannya... yang perlu diberikan demi membenarkan harga premium tersebut? Karena Anda tak bisa bilang, "Oh, ide bagus, Sabri. Biar kupatok harga dua, tiga kali lipat 'tuk barangku sekarang.". Karena transaksi terjadi waktu nilai melampaui harga! Dan dari pengalaman menangani ribuan klien di seluruh dunia, memberikan pengalaman pelanggan yang luar biasa itu tercipta dengan melakukan banyak hal-hal kecil di sepanjang perjalanan klien yang akan memperkuat pengalaman keseluruhan yang diberikan. Dan persis seperti itulah cara merek mewah ini bekerja. Mereka tahu secara pasti siapa nama Anda, serta barang apa saja yang pernah Anda beli sebelumnya. Karena itulah satu-satunya cara agar Anda bisa benar-benar mendapatkan produk yang ingin Anda beli sejak awal. Mereka tidak ada di sana hanya untuk menjual satu buah tas atau sabuk saja. Mereka mau Anda membeli dari mereka secara berulang kali selamanya. Dan begitulah cara mereka mendapatkan laba yang luar biasa tinggi ini. Jadi itulah strategi 'tuk tingkatkan laba Anda secara drastis. Sukai, *subscribe*, dan sampai jumpa di video berikutnya.

- The Psychology Behind Why We Buy Luxury Goods: Kind: captions Language: en purchasing items is a lot more complicated than you might imagine the mere act of buying something is often influenced by in this video we'll be investigating the psychology of super expensive items with a huge price tag slapped on several items it only makes sense that a certain class of people goes for the high-end items but that is not often the case consumers are not always rational with their purchases all at the time someone rational will act following the size of his or her pockets within reason and logic however several behavioral studies show that humans are not rational beings at all these studies also show that many consumers purchase luxury goods even when they're not in a position to do so the proof of this school of thought is evident in the high rates of consumer debt that many americans deal with no matter how you look at it it's pretty clear that many consumers do not act in their best people were still opt for the high-end luxury bag that will set them back thousands of dollars it does not matter that both bags perform the same function and are relatively the same in terms of quality one of the reasons why people would rather purchase high-end and expensive items over their cheaper counterparts is the human tendency to over emphasize the positive elements of a product and ignore the disadvantages a good example of this phenomenon is consumers of the products made by apple although there is no proof that the new releases of iphones ipads and mac computers released by apple are technologically superior or even unique when compared to their counterparts many people wait for overnight releases and spend hundreds and thousands of dollars purchasing their products this is even though there are cheaper options available that offer the same function in fact in terms of technological advancement and superiority many tech experts would choose samsung phones over iphones because those phones do not have the luxury brand that apple carries non-luxury goods are often considered inferior by so many people under them being non-luxury this perception has nothing to do with the characteristics and qualities of the products themselves this perception then leads to a warped conclusion that the higher priced goods are better quality this irrational logic leads them to believe that with higher priced goods what you pay for is what you get disregarding whether or not the goods are better than their more affordable counterparts costly items and expensive goods are often considered to be high quality exclusive custom-made goods that offer more to their consumers than their cheaper counterparts even though there is no proof that expensive means better many people would still consider expensive things to be better and more effective even when they are no different from their cheaper counterparts according to a study conducted by scientists at the california institute of technology and stanford united there are changes to the human brain when they discover they are making use of expensive items the study showed that people believe that the taste of wine is better when they're told it is expensive an mri scan of the brain revealed that they do enjoy it more when they think that the wine is indeed more expensive similar results were observed in another study when a placebo was used in place of an analgesic the participants who took the pill that were supposedly worth two dollars and fifty cents said that they felt more pain relief than when they took the pill worth only ten cents however both pills were identical placebos another reason why people would break the bank to purchase super expensive items can be associated with self-esteem low self-esteem can prompt people to purchase luxury items whether or not they can afford them several consumers associate owning luxury goods with boosting their self-esteem and giving them a sense of belonging with online shopping on the rise many people can purchase a 500 scarf at the click of a button whether or not they can afford it as many people consider shopping therapeutic luxury goods are they cannot normally afford the consumers would rather choose luxury items over the normal reasonably priced quality counterparts rather than save money with a fake rolex many people would rather pay will do the trick just fine well according to researchers at vale the quest for authenticity is something humans developed since childhood a study conducted tried to convince children that a cloning machine had produced their favorite toy the study discovered that most children refused to accept the duplicate as the original it was discovered that the sentimentality of the item is one of the reasons why we seek authenticity the sentimentality can occur in the form of memory or feeling that comes from having purchased a genuine luxury good that means that for some people treating themselves to a pair of fake christian louboutin brand boots is just not the same and not at all as special according to research conducted by michael norton a psychologist and professor of business administration at harvard business school price and perception have a huge influence on purchasing decisions of consumers if an item is reportedly twice as expensive it is assumed to be twice as good norton's research indicated that as humans we are motivated to splurge because our happiness can be boosted by having new experiences this is why many people might choose a five-star restaurant for a new experience rather than the safe and predictable three-star option it is also the reason why people choose to save up and go out all splurging during events like birthdays and anniversaries following that line of logic norton analyzed why people are constantly purchasing very expensive items and new expensive experiences there is an extra boost when you increase the quality of the - Why Luxury Cars Are Unreliable And Expensive | BMW, Mercedes, And More: Kind: captions Language: en why exactly are some luxury brands such as bmw mercedes and land rover known to be unreliable and expensive to own and what do you need to know before buying one of these cars that's what we're going to find out welcome to car help corner where we help you the consumer master the process of car buying and car ownership now i don't think it's a big secret that a lot of luxury brands especially european ones like bmw mercedes land rover and audi are known to be extremely expensive cars to own but why exactly are these companies known to be unreliable long term have high repair costs and what do you need to know before buying one of these cars well make sure to stick around to the end of the video because that's what we're going to find out after over 10 years as a professional car consultant helping consumers with all kinds of correlated issues i've received thousands of complaints from owners of various different car brands over all kinds of different serious repair issues and there's no question about it over that period of time you definitely start to see patterns in terms of which cars are more problematic than others of course i also consult with mechanics on a regular basis who work on these cars and look at the data from reputable organizations as well and when you put all this information together you start to see which car companies are the most problematic unreliable and expensive to own long term and the ones that consistently come at the very top are luxury european brands such as bmw mercedes audi and land rover of course there are a few other ones as well including porsche volvo and jaguar all of these companies basically fall into the same category of being extremely expensive cars to own long term well past the warranty period now of course european luxury brands are not the only ones that are known for reliability issues you can have all kinds of issues with a lot of domestic brands too including ford jeep gm and a few others but the big difference is going to be the cost of repair because european luxury cars just take repair cost to a whole new level and what do i mean by expensive to own well you can pretty much guarantee that anytime one of these cars develops a serious issue it's going to cost you at least four figures to fix if not more something as simple as a rough running engine with an engine misfire that involves replacing spark plugs ignition coils or fuel injectors can easily cost one to two thousand dollars on any one of these cars and if the engine has any serious oil leaks and requires a full resealing you can easily double that number and if you experience total engine failure on something like a bmw or mercedes a new remanufactured engine can cost you anywhere from twenty thousand dollars to upwards of sixty thousand dollars depending on the model you have i'm not joking it can actually cost the price of a whole brand new car and it's not just the engines you need to watch out for almost any of the parts on any one of these luxury brands can cost an enormous amount of money if you need to replace the full air suspension system on a range rover for example that can cost you as much as eight thousand dollars one example that i always remember though was from a client of mine who had a bmw that was just a warranty and it needed a new headlight assembly it was an expensive laser headlight unit and the cost of a replacement unit was just over sixteen thousand i was just as shocked as he was it's no surprise really that the value of a lot of these european luxury cars is basically tied to how much warranty coverage that they have once the warranty period ends the values begin to drop extremely fast that's because a lot of people know how expensive they are to repair so they're scared of buying one without a warranty now it's important to mention that a lot of these luxury european brands are actually fairly reliable for the first four to five years of ownership during the warranty period it's only when they become much older beyond seven to eight years old when they start to cost a lot of money in terms of repair issues so if you're only planning on buying one of these cars for a short term period then you don't have a lot to be concerned about so why exactly are a lot of luxury european brands so unreliable as they age well believe it or not there was a time period when that wasn't always the case back in the 1970s and 1980s companies such as mercedes and bmw were actually very well known for their engineering and build quality a lot of the cars they made were actually fairly durable and long lasting and those were qualities that they were long known for it was only when the 90s rolled around that a lot of that began to change fairly quickly and a lot of it had to do with globalization because the competition in the market was increasing so fast car brands were under a lot of pressure to offer a lot more for a lot less so we started to see a lot more cost cutting downsizing outsourcing all of which impacted the reliability of their cars luxury cars were also becoming a lot more complicated than ever before with more advanced engine technology computers sensors and other things that were not only making the cars a lot more complicated but also a lot more expensive to repair the final nail in the coffin though for reliability was when a lot of these luxury brands began reducing the amount of maintenance that they were requiring for their cars back in the 80s for example luxury brands like bmw and mercedes had pretty intense maintenance requirements and they required things like oil changes every 7 500 miles in addition to transmission fluid coolants and other fluid changes on a regular basis all of that completely changed though in the 90s when these companies began dramatically stretching their maintenance intervals recommending oil changes every 15 000 miles or 25 000 kilometers and completely eliminating other fluid changes like transmission fluid and coolant one of the reasons this was done was to reduce the perceived maintenance costs of their cars and make it look like they were less expensive and easier to maintain and what's the easiest way to do that just stretch out the maintenance intervals or eliminate them altogether now again a lot of this is not a big deal if you're buying a luxury car to own for a short term ownership period of around three to five years when the car is under warranty and you're not going to have to worry about expensive repairs or spending a lot of money on maintenance if you do plan to buy a luxury car from a european brand such as bmw mercedes or audi there are a lot of things that you can do to make sure that you don't get burned and end up spending an enormous amount of money in operating costs the first thing you might want to consider doing is to just lease one of these cars rather than buying it when you lease a car you're only going to have it for around three to four years under the warranty period so you're not gonna have to worry about expensive repair issues the best part about leasing one of these cars is not only are you going to get to enjoy a nice expensive luxury car but just before it's ready to have any serious problems it's ready to go back to the dealership if you would rather buy one of these cars though than leasing it then you're going to want to make sure that you get a really good extended warranty plan to protect yourself like i touched on earlier in the video the value of these luxury european brands is really tied to the amount of warranty coverage that they have once the warranty ends a lot of people are aware that these cars cost a lot of money to keep on the road so having that warranty is a huge benefit getting a good extended warranty plan for one of these luxury cars can easily cost you around three to four thousand depending on the level of coverage that you get but when a headlight can cost sixteen thousand dollars to replace i'd say it's money well spent if you're thinking about buying one of these european luxury cars on the used car market say one that's around three to four years old the best thing to do is to buy one from a new car dealership new car dealers often have a pretty good selection of lightly used cars that they typically receive on trade-in from owners who are just looking to upgrade to a new car and what they often do is they keep the best examples for themselves to sell and anything that's not worth keeping often just goes off to an auction where it later gets picked up by a used car dealership any high-end luxury car that ends up on a used car lot probably has some kind of serious backstory that you're not going to want to get involved in so just to play it safe buying from a new car dealership is usually the safest way to go the other important thing that you're going to want to know buying any high-end luxury car is whether it comes with service records and how well it's been maintained if you're buying something like a bmw or mercedes that doesn't have any kind of service information you don't know how often it's received oil changes and when it might experience a really serious problem having that full service history and knowing that the car has been maintained properly is extremely important when buying any high-end luxury car and you don't want to buy one without that although a lot of high-end luxury cars are extremely nice cars to own and drive there's no question that you're taking a much higher risk in terms of reliability issues and repair costs so if you're someone who just can't tolerate spending money on repairs and having problems then your best bet is to just buy something else the good news is that there are a few luxury brands that don't have a lot of these issues companies like lexus and acura are known for their - She Spent $50,000 on Designer Bags and Lost Everything: Kind: captions Language: en Emily Harper was a 28-year-old marketing coordinator living in a tiny Soho apartment in New York City. She had just received a $15,000 bonus after landing a big campaign for a beauty brand. That Friday afternoon, she walked into the Hermes store on Madison Avenue just to look. The moment she touched the soft Togo leather of a Birkin bag, something inside her shifted. It felt like success. It felt like she had finally made it. Over the next 8 months, Emily spent $50,000 on luxury handbags. She bought a classic Chanel flap for $12,000, a limited edition Louis Vuitton for $8,500, two Dior saddle bags, and several Gucci and Prada pieces she saw influencers carrying on Instagram. She photographed every bag in perfect lighting, posted them with captions like, "Treat yourself, queen." And, "Living my best life." And watched her follower count climb to 45K. Her colleagues envied her. Her friends called her the bag lady. Rent was $4,800 a month. Credit card bills arrived, but Emily barely glanced at them. The thrill of unboxing a new bag was stronger than any fear of debt. She told herself, "I work hard. I deserve this." By the end of the year, Emily owned 18 designer bags, but her financial life was collapsing. Credit card debt had ballooned to $68,000 with high interest rates. She started missing payments. Late fees piled up. Work suffered, too. She was distracted, constantly checking resale sites during meetings, and staying up late scrolling luxury drops. Her boss noticed the drop in performance and cut her bonus. One rainy morning, Emily received a final warning from the bank. Her accounts would be frozen. Panic set in. She tried selling a few bags online, but panic selling meant she lost money on most of them. Eventually, she had no choice but to file for chapter 7 bankruptcy. In court, the judge reviewed her expenses and shook his head. "50,000 dollars on handbags while your rent and bills went unpaid?" Emily moved back to her parents' house in New Jersey. She sold almost everything. Her once glamorous Instagram sat silent. The last post she made was a simple black screen with white text. "I spent 50,000 dollars chasing luxury and ended up with nothing but regret. Real wealth isn't in logos. It's in peace of mind." She learned the hardest way possible. Shiny bags couldn't fill the emptiness, and status bought with debt always comes with a devastating price.

- The Battle for Your Time: Exposing the Costs of Social Media | Dino Ambrosi | TEDxLagunaBlancaSchool: Kind: captions Language: en Transcriber: Norhan Eliwa Reviewer: Sadegh Vahdati Nia By providing space for constant evolution, we can all transform how we view ourselves and the world around us. Bear with me, everybody. I'm going to start off today on a little bit of a heavy note, but I promise things will lighten up. The dots on this screen represent an adult life in months, assuming a life expectancy of 90. So if you're 18 years old right now, this is an optimistic estimate of the months that you have left. Take a second to take that in. Probably not as many as you would expect. And I’m sorry to say that it does get worse because about a third of that time is going to be spent sleeping. On average, 126 of those months will go to school in your career. About 18 will be spent driving, 36 cooking and eating, 36 doing chores and errands and about 27 in the bathroom and taking care of personal hygiene. So that leaves you with 334 months, optimistically, for everything else. So this is where you tick the boxes on your bucket list. This is where you pursue your passions and travel the world and leave your mark. How you spend this time is going to determine the quality of your life. But this time isn't just something that you spend, it’s also something that you invest. Because what you do with it will quite literally determine the kind of person you become. The body, mind and character that you will have in the future are being actively shaped by how you choose to use your time today. So take a second and ask yourself, “What do you want to do with that free time? What things do you want to do that you haven't done? Who do you want to spend that time with? What is worth investing it in?” Now, I would be willing to bet that scrolling through TikTok, binge watching Netflix, and playing video games probably did not come to mind. But today, the average 18 year old in the United States is on pace to spend 93% of their remaining free time looking at a screen. That is not counting time for school. So wrap your head around how sad that is. Imagine getting to the age of 90, seeing this visualization of how you spent all your time after the age of 18 and thinking about all the things you could have done that you did not do because you got distracted. And I also want you to ask yourself, “What do you think over 26 years of screen time would do to you? What is that an investment in? How would it change you?” It's well established that there's a link between high screen time and mental health issues such as depression and anxiety. But recently we've started to unveil the cognitive consequences of excessive tech use as well. When we're staring at our screens, we are constantly switching our attention between different pieces of information. The average TikTok is about 15 seconds long, and over 55% of webpages are viewed for 15 seconds or less. And if you're switching your attention every 15 seconds for an average of eight hours and 39 minutes a day, you are training yourself to become chronically distracted. Think about what that will do to your career, to your relationships, and to your ability to pursue the things that matter most to you. Unfortunately, the consequences of screen time are not limited to our mental health and our cognition. Because every social media platform carries a message that affects what we believe. They influence the way we see ourselves and the way we see the world purely based on how they are designed. Instagram inherently says that your worth is largely defined by what you look like and what you do on vacations. It compels you to capture all the most meaningful moments of your life on camera and share them with your entire social network. And it implicitly says that it's more valuable to have a thousand people that will give you transient social approval than a few that deeply care about you, even when it’s not your best day. Snapchat inherently says that the quality of our relationships is best measured by the frequency of our communication, regardless of what we're actually saying. You get a point added to your Snapchat streak, even if you just send a picture of the side of your face with the caption streaks. Twitter says that anything worth saying can and should be reduced to an arbitrary number of characters. It says that the world is black and white. That it's more important to be updated about everything, then deeply informed about anything. And when you start to compare the messages these platforms are sending with those of technologies from the past, you begin to get a sense of what we might be losing, because the inherent structure of a book says that the world is complex and it takes time to understand. It compels us to walk in the shoes of other people and see things from their perspective with context. And it forces us to focus on one train of thought for an extended period of time, which nurtures our attention. And the letter tells us that our communication doesn't need to be frequent. It just needs to be deep. So when you factor all that in, it quickly becomes clear that the opportunity cost of this screen time is impossible to calculate. And I have never shown this visualization to anyone that actually wants to spend time staring at a screen. So there is a stark difference between how much time we say our screens are worth and how much time we actually give them. And it is critical to realize that is not an accident. That is by design. It’s a consequence of a business model, that has incentives which are fundamentally misaligned with your wellbeing. Because you are the product that social media sells. These services are free because they are monetizing you. They profit by helping advertisers change your future behavior, whether that be where you spend your time, how you spend your money or even who you vote for. In order to do that, they have to do two things. They need to figure out which ads are going to influence you by collecting as much data about you as they possibly can. And then they need to show you as many of those ads as possible. So social media is free because you pay for it with your time. Their profit is directly linked to how long they can get you to scroll. And every social media platform is in a battle with each other to capture as much of your free time as possible. So let’s run a thought experiment. I want you to ask yourself, how much would you pay for your favorite social media platform if it charged you a monthly subscription fee? So pick the app that you use the most. And raise your hand if you would pay at least $5 a month. Okay. How about $10 a month? 20? Don't see any hands anymore. Well, let's do a quick calculation to figure out how much we're effectively paying for an app like TikTok. We'll assume that you value your time at a rate of $20 per hour and you're spending two hours a day on the platform or 30 days in a month. So you're effectively paying $1,200 per month for TikTok. So when you start to do this kind of analysis, it quickly becomes clear that most of us are drastically overpaying for social media. My ask of you is this: Figure out what it means for you to get a good deal out of social media platforms. In order to do that, you have to do two things: You have to ask yourself, “What value do these services provide? And second, you have to ask, “How much of your time is that value worth?” Now, I want to be clear that I am not saying social media is without value. It can be an incredibly powerful tool. It can foster relationships. It can introduce you to new ideas. It can even spark social movements. But we need to learn to use it in moderation. Don't let yourself get to the age of 90, only to look back on your life and realize that while you were trying to avoid FOMO, you actually missed out on living. That free time is your most valuable resource. Do not give it away for free. Thank you. (Applause)

- Minimalism vs  Consumerism - Advanced (C1) ESL Listening: Kind: captions Language: en-IE Minimalism vs. Consumerism In today’s fast-paced world,&nbsp;&nbsp; two opposing lifestyles often come into focus:&nbsp; minimalism and consumerism. They represent not&nbsp;&nbsp; only different ways of living but also distinct&nbsp; attitudes toward happiness, success, and identity.&nbsp; Consumerism, which dominates much of modern&nbsp; society, is based on the idea that more is better.&nbsp;&nbsp; Advertisements, social media, and cultural&nbsp; expectations often persuade people that&nbsp;&nbsp; happiness lies in the next purchase—be it a&nbsp; new phone, fashionable clothes, or the latest&nbsp;&nbsp; car. Shopping malls are not just places to&nbsp; buy necessities; they are temples of desire,&nbsp;&nbsp; designed to stimulate impulse buying and create&nbsp; a sense of urgency. For many, owning more items&nbsp;&nbsp; provides a feeling of status and security,&nbsp; even if this satisfaction is often temporary.&nbsp; Minimalism, on the other hand, challenges this&nbsp; belief. Minimalists argue that true fulfillment&nbsp;&nbsp; comes not from accumulating possessions, but&nbsp; from reducing clutter and focusing on what&nbsp;&nbsp; really matters. By intentionally owning&nbsp; fewer things, minimalists seek clarity,&nbsp;&nbsp; freedom, and peace of mind. Their homes are&nbsp; not empty but carefully curated, with objects&nbsp;&nbsp; that bring genuine joy or serve a practical&nbsp; purpose. For them, time, relationships, and&nbsp;&nbsp; experiences are more valuable than material goods. The contrast between these two lifestyles raises&nbsp;&nbsp; important questions. Is consumerism simply a&nbsp; natural result of economic progress, or is it an&nbsp;&nbsp; unhealthy cycle of desire and dissatisfaction?&nbsp; Does minimalism represent a genuine path&nbsp;&nbsp; to happiness, or is it a privilege available&nbsp; only to those who can afford to reject excess?&nbsp; From a psychological perspective, minimalism is&nbsp; often linked with reduced stress, improved focus,&nbsp;&nbsp; and greater financial stability. People&nbsp; who adopt minimalist practices frequently&nbsp;&nbsp; report feeling less overwhelmed and more&nbsp; in control of their lives. Consumerism,&nbsp;&nbsp; however, is closely tied to debt, anxiety,&nbsp; and environmental harm, as constant production&nbsp;&nbsp; and waste take their toll on the planet. Yet, it is important to acknowledge the nuance.&nbsp;&nbsp; Consumerism is not inherently negative;&nbsp; buying goods and services fuels economies,&nbsp;&nbsp; creates jobs, and provides comfort. Likewise,&nbsp; minimalism is not about complete deprivation.&nbsp;&nbsp; It is about intentional living—choosing&nbsp; carefully rather than endlessly consuming.&nbsp; Ultimately, the debate between minimalism and&nbsp; consumerism is not about strict rules but about&nbsp;&nbsp; awareness. Both lifestyles invite individuals&nbsp; to ask: What do I really need to be happy?&nbsp;&nbsp; For some, joy may come from a simple&nbsp; home, a few meaningful possessions,&nbsp;&nbsp; and rich experiences. For others, it may come from&nbsp; embracing the abundance of modern life. Perhaps&nbsp;&nbsp; the wisest choice lies in balance—knowing&nbsp; when to consume and when to step back. I believe minimalism leads to greater&nbsp; happiness than consumerism because it&nbsp;&nbsp; helps people focus on what really matters.&nbsp; Consumerism constantly pushes us to want more,&nbsp;&nbsp; but the satisfaction of buying something&nbsp; new often disappears quickly. In contrast,&nbsp;&nbsp; minimalism creates a sense of peace.&nbsp; When people live with fewer possessions,&nbsp;&nbsp; they can concentrate on experiences,&nbsp; relationships, and personal growth.&nbsp; However, I don’t think consumerism is entirely&nbsp; negative. Buying things allows us to enjoy&nbsp;&nbsp; comfort, technology, and services that improve&nbsp; our lives. The problem comes when shopping&nbsp;&nbsp; becomes the main source of happiness. Minimalism&nbsp; reminds us that objects are not everything.&nbsp; In my opinion, the healthiest approach is&nbsp; balance. We should appreciate the benefits&nbsp;&nbsp; of consumer goods but avoid overconsumption.&nbsp; At the same time, we should learn to value&nbsp;&nbsp; simplicity and understand that happiness&nbsp; often comes from things money cannot buy.

  Top Comments:
  1. @EnglishAudioStories-o5g [1 likes]: Minimalism vs. Consumerism 

In today’s fast-paced world, two opposing lifestyles often come into focus: minimalism and consumerism. They represent not only different ways of living but also distinct attitudes toward happiness, success, and identity.
Consumerism, which dominates much of modern society, is based on the idea that more is better. Advertisements, social media, and cultural expectations often persuade people that happiness lies in the next purchase—be it a new phone, fashionable clothes, or the latest car. Shopping malls are not just places to buy necessities; they are temples of desire, designed to stimulate impulse buying and create a sense of urgency. For many, owning more items provides a feeling of status and security, even if this satisfaction is often temporary.
Minimalism, on the other hand, challenges this belief. Minimalists argue that true fulfillment comes not from accumulating possessions, but from reducing clutter and focusing on what really matters. By intentionally owning fewer things, minimalists seek clarity, freedom, and peace of mind. Their homes are not empty but carefully curated, with objects that bring genuine joy or serve a practical purpose. For them, time, relationships, and experiences are more valuable than material goods.
The contrast between these two lifestyles raises important questions. Is consumerism simply a natural result of economic progress, or is it an unhealthy cycle of desire and dissatisfaction? Does minimalism represent a genuine path to happiness, or is it a privilege available only to those who can afford to reject excess?
From a psychological perspective, minimalism is often linked with reduced stress, improved focus, and greater financial stability. People who adopt minimalist practices frequently report feeling less overwhelmed and more in control of their lives. Consumerism, however, is closely tied to debt, anxiety, and environmental harm, as constant production and waste take their toll on the planet.
Yet, it is important to acknowledge the nuance. Consumerism is not inherently negative; buying goods and services fuels economies, creates jobs, and provides comfort. Likewise, minimalism is not about complete deprivation. It is about intentional living—choosing carefully rather than endlessly consuming.
Ultimately, the debate between minimalism and consumerism is not about strict rules but about awareness. Both lifestyles invite individuals to ask: What do I really need to be happy? For some, joy may come from a simple home, a few meaningful possessions, and rich experiences. For others, it may come from embracing the abundance of modern life. Perhaps the wisest choice lies in balance—knowing when to consume and when to step back.

 Comprehension Questions 

1.	What is consumerism mainly based on?

4.	How do minimalists usually describe their homes?

7.	What are some negative effects linked to consumerism?
8.	Why is consumerism not considered entirely negative?
9.	What is minimalism not about, according to the passage?
10.	What is the central question that both lifestyles invite individuals to ask?

 Answers

1.	Consumerism is based on the idea that more possessions equal more happiness.
2.	They persuade people that happiness lies in the next purchase and create urgency to buy.
3.	True fulfillment comes from reducing clutter and focusing on what truly matters.
4.	Minimalists describe their homes as carefully curated with meaningful or practical objects.
5.	Whether consumerism is a natural result of progress or an unhealthy cycle, and whether minimalism is genuine happiness or a privilege.
6.	Reduced stress, improved focus, and greater financial stability.
7.	Debt, anxiety, and environmental harm.
8.	Because it fuels economies, creates jobs, and provides comfort.
9.	Minimalism is not about deprivation, but about intentional living.
10.	“What do I really need to be happy?”

 Discussion Questions 

1.	Do you think society today is more consumerist or minimalist? Why?
2.	How does social media affect the way people spend money?
3.	Would you consider yourself closer to a minimalist or consumerist lifestyle?
4.	What role do advertisements play in shaping our desires?
5.	Is minimalism possible for everyone, or is it a lifestyle of privilege?
6.	How can consumerism harm the environment?
7.	Can consumerism ever make people truly happy? Why or why not?
8.	Do you believe balance between the two lifestyles is the best option?
9.	What possessions are truly indispensable in your life?
10.	How would your life change if you gave up half of your belongings?

 Writing Task

Write a 200–250 word essay answering the question:
“Do you think minimalism leads to greater happiness than consumerism? Why or why not?”

 Example Essay

I believe minimalism leads to greater happiness than consumerism because it helps people focus on what really matters. Consumerism constantly pushes us to want more, but the satisfaction of buying something new often disappears quickly. In contrast, minimalism creates a sense of peace. When people live with fewer possessions, they can concentrate on experiences, relationships, and personal growth.
However, I don’t think consumerism is entirely negative. Buying things allows us to enjoy comfort, technology, and services that improve our lives. The problem comes when shopping becomes the main source of happiness. Minimalism reminds us that objects are not everything.
In my opinion, the healthiest approach is balance. We should appreciate the benefits of consumer goods but avoid overconsumption. At the same time, we should learn to value simplicity and understand that happiness often comes from things money cannot buy.

For those of you that aren't familiar, painoint marketing is essentially a type of marketing that people will use to sell their services. And this isn't specific to the coaching industry. It's like any kind of product or service. Basically drawing attention to some sort of struggle that you're facing. So often in coaching, it might be like, "Are you not getting any clients?" Or, "Are you sick of not making a gajillion dollars?" And it works because obviously if you are someone that's struggling with that thing, you're going to feel seen, you're going to feel heard, which is one of the key principles of effective marketing. And so then the idea behind painoint marketing is I help you to acknowledge whatever that struggle is that you're facing and then I position myself as the like perfect solution to help you solve that problem. Now, what I think we see in coaching specifically is that this type of marketing gets overused like an unbelievable amount. And so, what it actually ends up doing is it attracts people that are typically in a state of desperation or extreme vulnerability who are seeking reprieve from their suffering. And then because of this, it positions the coach or the mentor as sort of this savior that is going to help them, you know, alleviate that suffering. And so it creates this sort of power imbalance in my opinion. And so for me personally, what I prefer to use or would recommend for people to use is something more along the lines of transformational marketing. And so what transformational marketing does in contrast is it focuses more on the possibility and less on the pain and the shame. And so instead of saying something like, "Are you tired of never getting any clients because you're a big loser?" You would say something like, "Imagine you had the right support to be able to build the systems to attract clients effortlessly or whatever the pitch is." And so the idea is that this type of marketing would inspire people versus make them feel shame or really lean into those not so great feelings where they're making a decision out of desperation. And so I don't know if that makes sense in the context of what she was saying in this video, but for me it's just painpoint marketing feels very 2016. - Luxury Handbag Overconsumption Has Gone Too Far: Kind: captions Language: en Across decades of pop culture, the handbag has been a picture of femininity. Not just a place to carry things, but a signifier. A Louis Vuitton monogram, a Chanel flap, an Airmes Birkin. The designer handbag in particular has become a cultural right of passage. To carry one is to signal that you've made it, that you're someone of status, of taste, of money, or at least someone who can convincingly perform it. Your first real purse, the kind with a conspicuous designer logo on it, often marks your entry into adult femininity. For some, it's a graduation gift.
